FERRET FRENZY
Eight-Role d6/d12 Gameplay Solidification Dossier
Revision 3.2 — Fourth Dossier Pass · Full-Package Preservation + Target-Legality Hardening
Prepared October 2026

DOCUMENT PURPOSE
================

This document is the gameplay-only revision of the eight-role Ferret Frenzy design. It uses the most
recent Ferret Frenzy dossier and the latest frontend package as the authority for the existing game
skeleton, then more heavily applies the design lessons documented in the supplied Cheese Thief and
Among Us research dossiers. It does not redesign background images, role-card art, the shell, lobby
styling, audio presentation, or backend endpoint. The eight roles remain Bandit, Dooker, Itchy,
Trouble, Business, Snuggler, Hunter, and Guardian. The two-die system remains exactly d6 plus d12.
The purpose is to make role behavior, evidence, deception, conversions, card movement, Morning
Business, and Paw Point resolution precise enough that humans and bots can play the same game
without interpretation gaps.

SOURCE BASIS. Source A is Ferret_Frenzy_8_Role_d6_d12_Revised_Dossier.txt, Revision 2.0. Source B is
Cheese_Thief_Extreme_Research_Dossier_50000_words.txt. Source C is
Among_Us_Mobile_Game_Extreme_Research_Dossier.txt. The latest
FerretFrenzy_ChatVoting_QuickClaims_BotPrivacy package was also inspected to confirm the currently
implemented role roster, role wake counts, current-card movement model, chat/claim boundaries, bot
information rules, simultaneous Paw Point behavior, and backend-neutral frontend expectations. Revision 3.1 retains Revision 3.0 as the role-and-interaction baseline and supersedes it only where the new implementation-reconciliation rules are more specific. Non-gameplay presentation work remains intentionally outside scope. The current Revision 3 frontend package was also re-audited for this pass, including its role-neutral deal flow, Quick Role Claim model, bot privacy behavior, card-state presentation, Paw Point UI, compatibility normalizer, and automated regression tests.


REVISION 3.1 PASS SUMMARY
-------------------------

This third dossier pass keeps the existing eight roles, d6+d12 system, Active Set recipes, historical Raider objective, and 28-pair interaction analysis intact. Its main change is a new Part IV that turns several previously implicit implementation assumptions into explicit rules. The new contracts define the repeated-lowest d6 procedure for an earliest-Bandit tie, target self-exclusion, moved-Current-Card information visibility, Itchy clue delivery when theft happens after both Itchy wakes, Hunter second-wake conversion/mark timing, Guardian's zero-capture edge case, Paw Point -> Hunt Mark -> Bond-Reveal result order, and a stricter bot information firewall.

The current package's automated tests pass, so this revision is not a rollback. It is a precision pass designed to prevent future frontend, backend, bot, reconnect, or replay code from resolving the same legal round differently. Where an existing implementation exposes more information than the human-facing rules authorize, Revision 3.1 treats the information boundary as the rule and the implementation as the item to correct.

AUTHORITATIVE NON-NEGOTIABLES
------------------------------

- Exactly eight named roles: Bandit, Dooker, Itchy, Trouble, Business, Snuggler, Hunter, Guardian.
- Exactly two mechanical dice: d12 for Burrow Hour; d6 for defined chance/tiebreak checks. No new die type is added.
- The fixed 24-card pool remains 4 Bandit, 14 Business, and one each of Dooker, Itchy, Trouble, Snuggler, Hunter, Guardian.
- A round uses one Starting Card per player plus three Hammock cards. Starting Role never changes and determines scheduled role actions. Current Card may move.
- Historical Raider is the starting Bandit who actually steals the Minnow Treats. Raider status is player-bound history and never moves with a card.
- Morning Business is shared discussion. Public claims are never auto-verified by the app. Private fact views may show only authorized confirmed facts.
- Paw Point is one simultaneous private vote per active player. Guardian protection resolves before vote capture. All eligible tied top vote-getters are caught.
- Business-side primary win condition is catching the historical Raider. Catching an accomplice or converted Hunter without the Raider is not enough.
- No Sheriff, Mayor, leader, police analogue, captain, tie-break authority, or ninth base role is introduced.
- Bots follow exactly the same information boundaries as human players; no omniscient deduction engine is allowed to leak server truth into public chat or voting.

REVISION 3.2 PASS NOTE. This fourth dossier pass is intentionally additive. It preserves the complete Revision 3.0 role deep dives, all twenty-eight pair interactions, the Revision 3.1 implementation-reconciliation contracts, and the full playable package used as the current baseline. Revision 3.2 does not authorize deleting code, images, audio, card art, bot response libraries, tests, documentation, or other working game content merely because a pass is focused on one subsystem. The package itself is part of the implementation evidence and must be carried forward unless a file is explicitly superseded by a tested replacement.


PART I — DESIGN INFLUENCE REBALANCE
===================================


1. Cheese Thief becomes the primary night-structure influence
-------------------------------------------------------------

Private Timestamps. Cheese Thief turns a die into a private time stamp. The important lesson is that
randomness creates an evidence graph rather than merely deciding success or failure. Ferret Frenzy
should translate this principle into the twelve-hour Burrow Clock rather than merely citing it as
inspiration. Every role must still care when it woke, what the Minnow Treats looked like at that
moment, and who shared that wake. A special role that can solve the round without engaging those
questions should be treated as a balance problem.

Before/After Object State. The presence or absence of the central stolen object gives every wake a
temporal meaning. A truthful “present” and truthful “missing” can bracket the crime even when
neither speaker knows the thief. Ferret Frenzy should translate this principle into the twelve-hour
Burrow Clock rather than merely citing it as inspiration. Every role must still care when it woke,
what the Minnow Treats looked like at that moment, and who shared that wake. A special role that can
solve the round without engaging those questions should be treated as a balance problem.

Collision And Solitude. Waking with other players creates witness relationships; waking alone can
create a different information privilege. The game therefore makes occupancy itself meaningful.
Ferret Frenzy should translate this principle into the twelve-hour Burrow Clock rather than merely
citing it as inspiration. Every role must still care when it woke, what the Minnow Treats looked
like at that moment, and who shared that wake. A special role that can solve the round without
engaging those questions should be treated as a balance problem.

Conversion Through The Night. Follower rules show that allegiance can change because of what
happened during the night. That makes old trust and new incentives coexist. Ferret Frenzy should
translate this principle into the twelve-hour Burrow Clock rather than merely citing it as
inspiration. Every role must still care when it woke, what the Minnow Treats looked like at that
moment, and who shared that wake. A special role that can solve the round without engaging those
questions should be treated as a balance problem.

Simultaneous Accusation. A simultaneous final vote protects the game from turn-order bullying and
creates tactical value in ties because more than one top suspect can be exposed. Ferret Frenzy
should translate this principle into the twelve-hour Burrow Clock rather than merely citing it as
inspiration. Every role must still care when it woke, what the Minnow Treats looked like at that
moment, and who shared that wake. A special role that can solve the round without engaging those
questions should be treated as a balance problem.

Short-Round Variance. Because a round is compact, some nights can be information-rich and others
sparse. The design does not need to guarantee a perfectly solved puzzle every round; it needs enough
structure that discussion is about something real. Ferret Frenzy should translate this principle
into the twelve-hour Burrow Clock rather than merely citing it as inspiration. Every role must still
care when it woke, what the Minnow Treats looked like at that moment, and who shared that wake. A
special role that can solve the round without engaging those questions should be treated as a
balance problem.


2. Among Us becomes the primary discussion/evidence influence
-------------------------------------------------------------

Evidence Becomes Testimony. Among Us repeatedly converts private spatial observations into public
claims during a meeting. Ferret Frenzy does the same with wake windows, Treat State, co-wakers, card
movement, and role facts. Ferret Frenzy replaces rooms/routes with Burrow Hours and replaces
bodies/sabotage with Treat State, role effects, conversion, and card movement. The social lesson
remains the same: software supplies incomplete observations; players turn them into testimony;
testimony is tested in a time-limited group discussion; the vote resolves the social contest.

Roles Reinterpret Evidence. The strongest role additions change what familiar evidence means instead
of replacing deduction. A special role should create an exception players can reason about, not a
button that prints the winner. Ferret Frenzy replaces rooms/routes with Burrow Hours and replaces
bodies/sabotage with Treat State, role effects, conversion, and card movement. The social lesson
remains the same: software supplies incomplete observations; players turn them into testimony;
testimony is tested in a time-limited group discussion; the vote resolves the social contest.

Ordinary Behavior Is Camouflage. Hostile players are strongest when they resemble the majority.
Ferret Frenzy therefore needs a strong ordinary Business claim structure that Bandits can imitate
plausibly. Ferret Frenzy replaces rooms/routes with Burrow Hours and replaces bodies/sabotage with
Treat State, role effects, conversion, and card movement. The social lesson remains the same:
software supplies incomplete observations; players turn them into testimony; testimony is tested in
a time-limited group discussion; the vote resolves the social contest.

False Certainty Is Dangerous. Confidence is not evidence. Genuine observation, mistaken inference,
stale information, and deliberate lying can all sound equally certain in discussion. Ferret Frenzy
replaces rooms/routes with Burrow Hours and replaces bodies/sabotage with Treat State, role effects,
conversion, and card movement. The social lesson remains the same: software supplies incomplete
observations; players turn them into testimony; testimony is tested in a time-limited group
discussion; the vote resolves the social contest.

Meeting Is The Contest. The decisive conflict is the discussion and vote. Night powers matter
because they shape what can be argued later; they should not resolve the social game before Morning
Business begins. Ferret Frenzy replaces rooms/routes with Burrow Hours and replaces bodies/sabotage
with Treat State, role effects, conversion, and card movement. The social lesson remains the same:
software supplies incomplete observations; players turn them into testimony; testimony is tested in
a time-limited group discussion; the vote resolves the social contest.

State-Changing Roles Contaminate Old Clears. When a role can convert, move cards, or change the
meaning of a familiar signal, a fact may remain true while the conclusion drawn from it becomes
false. This is central to Hunter/Guardian and Dooker/Trouble interactions. Ferret Frenzy replaces
rooms/routes with Burrow Hours and replaces bodies/sabotage with Treat State, role effects,
conversion, and card movement. The social lesson remains the same: software supplies incomplete
observations; players turn them into testimony; testimony is tested in a time-limited group
discussion; the vote resolves the social contest.


3. Evidence taxonomy for Ferret Frenzy
--------------------------------------

HARD PERSONAL FACT: information delivered by the authoritative game specifically to this player: own
die result, Treat State, co-waker identity, legal scan result, legal inspection result, conversion
result, bond identity, starting-role recognition, or own target choice.

HARD HISTORICAL EVENT: theft occurred, a Current Card moved between named positions, a player-bound
status was created, or a final protection/vote/catch event happened. The event may be hidden during
play but is objective in replay.

CORROBORATED CLAIM: two or more independent public stories agree about a mechanically checkable
fact, such as sharing Hour 6. Corroboration raises confidence but is not server verification because
hostile players can coordinate.

UNCORROBORATED CLAIM: a public statement no other current evidence confirms. It may be true,
mistaken, selectively true, or false.

INFERENCE: a conclusion derived from facts and claims, such as “the raid was probably Hours 5–7” or
“this player is most likely Raider.” Inferences are never shown as facts by the UI.

STALE FACT: a statement that was mechanically true at an earlier timestamp but may not describe dawn
state, such as Dooker’s allegiance Glimpse before later card movement or Guardian’s starting-Hunter
recognition after possible conversion.

SOCIAL READ: behavioral interpretation such as confidence, hesitation, coalition behavior, or
consistency of phrasing. It is legal evidence for human play but receives no authoritative system
weight.


PART II — SOLIDIFIED ROUND FLOW
===============================

Stage 1: Active Set and deal. Build the player-count recipe plus three Hammock cards from the fixed
pool, shuffle authoritatively, deal one Starting Card per occupied seat, keep three hidden Hammock
cards. Bots never reserve roles.

Stage 2: Pre-night relationships. Snuggler chooses two other players to bond. If both Hunter and
Guardian are player-held Starting Roles, they recognize one another now. These relationships attach
to people and survive later card movement.

Stage 3: Private dice. Players roll the d12 wake schedule required by Starting Role; duplicate
results for a multi-wake player reroll only the duplicate until unique. Itchy rolls d6 for
activation. Earliest starting Bandit becomes Raider, with private lowest-d6 tiebreak among tied
earliest Bandits.

Stage 4: Twelve Burrow Hours. At each hour: wake scheduled players; show Treat State before action;
resolve Raider theft if this is Raider hour; resolve witness/conversion triggers; resolve scheduled
Starting-Role actions; commit state; return non-entitled players to Night Lock.

Stage 5: Morning Business. Freeze night action. Players discuss hours, Treat State, co-wakers, role
claims, card movement, bond/trust claims, and possible conversions. App does not publish guilt
scores or validate claims.

Stage 6: Guardian protection. Immediately before Paw Point lock, Guardian may protect one other
player. Protection removes that player from vote-capture eligibility for this resolution only.

Stage 7: Simultaneous Paw Point. Each active player selects one other eligible player as their
private vote. All votes lock simultaneously. Remove protected targets from capture eligibility, find
the highest remaining eligible total, and catch every player tied at that total.

Stage 8: Secondary resolution and primary win. Resolve Bond-Reveal and loyal Hunter Hunt Mark. Then
check whether historical Raider is in the final catch set. Business wins if yes; Bandit side wins if
Raider escapes. Secondary personal outcomes are displayed separately.


Same-hour event order
---------------------

Authoritative order within one Burrow Hour is: (1) scheduled players wake and see co-wakers; (2)
Treat State at the start of the window is shown; (3) historical Raider steals if this is its theft
hour and treats are still present; (4) theft-witness conversion triggers resolve, including Trouble
In On It and Hunter's first applicable overlap conversion check; (5) Starting-Role actions scheduled
for that wake resolve; (6) state is committed and player-private facts are delivered. This order
means a Dooker Glimpse in the theft hour can observe an allegiance after a conversion that occurred
moments earlier in the same window. It also means Trouble converts before performing its blind swap
in that window.


Current allegiance precedence
-----------------------------

Revision 3.0 treats allegiance as a separate derived field with an explicit precedence rule. First,
the historical Raider is Bandit-side regardless of Current Card. Second, a player with the player-
bound Trouble In On It status or Hunter-converted status is Bandit-side regardless of Current Card.
Third, for everyone else, a Bandit Current Card means Bandit-side and any non-Bandit Current Card
means Business-side. This is evaluated for dawn allegiance and for a Dooker Glimpse at the exact
timestamp of that Glimpse. Current Card never grants the historical knowledge that belonged to a
Starting Role. A player who receives a Bandit card through movement does not retroactively join the
starting Bandit roster, does not learn the Raider identity from setup, and does not become the
historical Raider. Conversely, a non-Raider starting Bandit whose card moves away can become
Business-side at dawn while still remembering the Bandit roster learned at setup; that awkward
knowledge conflict is intentional one-night-style deduction, not a reset of memory.


Business solo inspection solidification
---------------------------------------

A Business player wakes alone only when no other active player is scheduled in that same Burrow Hour
at the beginning of the wake. If lone, Business chooses one other player and inspects exactly one
wake result belonging to that player. If the target has multiple d12 wake results, the game reveals
one result selected uniformly from the target's scheduled results and does not disclose how many
other results exist. This preserves the Cheese Thief lone-wake peek idea while preventing Business
from turning one inspection into a complete schedule scan of Dooker, Hunter, or activated Itchy.


Itchy candidate-clue solidification
-----------------------------------

When Itchy activates on d6 5–6, the app generates its special clue only after the historical Raider
has committed the theft. The clue contains exactly two active player identities: the historical
Raider plus one non-Raider decoy whenever at least one legal decoy exists. The decoy is chosen uniformly from active non-Raider players other than Itchy so the choice itself
does not leak an extra hidden weighting rule. The app never labels which name is Raider. Card
movement after theft does not alter the historical candidate guarantee.


Snuggle Bond solidification
---------------------------

Snuggler chooses two other players, never self, before Hour 1. The two bonded players learn each
other's identity as bonded partners but receive no alignment or role scan. If one bonded player is
caught by Paw Point or a successful loyal-Hunter Hunt Mark, the partner becomes Bond-Revealed in
final results. Bond-Reveal exposes that partner's result information but is not itself a Paw Point
catch and does not by itself satisfy the Business primary win if the revealed partner is historical
Raider. This wording resolves earlier ambiguity between 'revealed' and 'caught' and keeps the bond
consequential without making a pre-night random relationship an automatic theft solution.


Guardian protection solidification
----------------------------------

Guardian selects one other player after Morning Business and before votes lock. The protected player
is ineligible to be caught by the Paw Point vote. Votes cast at the protected player remain recorded
for replay but are excluded when computing the highest eligible capture total. The game then catches
every unprotected eligible player tied at the highest remaining total. Protection does not block
Dooker, Trouble, Itchy, bond, conversion, or post-vote reveal logic and is never evidence that the
protected player is innocent.


Historical Raider is the primary mystery
----------------------------------------

Ferret Frenzy may create additional Bandit-aligned players through starting Bandits, Current Card
movement, Trouble conversion, or Hunter conversion, but the primary round question stays simple: who
actually took the Minnow Treats? Business wins only when the historical Raider is caught through the
final capture logic. This is the strongest Cheese Thief inheritance and prevents the game from
degenerating into 'catch any hostile.' It also lets special-role interactions remain complicated
while the headline objective remains teachable.


PART III — EIGHT ROLE DEEP DIVES
================================


ROLE DEEP DIVE: BANDIT
======================

Role charter. Bandit is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: Bandit side. Wake structure: 1 d12. d6 use: only Raider tiebreak when
earliest Bandits tie. The solidification pass does not replace this role with an Among Us or Cheese
Thief role; it uses those games as design references for how evidence, deception, conversion, and
voting should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: Starting Bandits know the starting Bandit roster.
The earliest starting-Bandit wake becomes the historical Raider and must steal the Minnow Treats in
that window. Other Bandits are accomplices whose job is to keep the Raider out of the final caught
set.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Bandit rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Bandit, the core action
contract is: The Raider performs the theft; non-Raider Bandits receive no extra mechanical power
beyond faction knowledge and ordinary wake evidence.

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Bandit, confirmed private
facts include own wake hour; starting Bandit roster; Raider identity; Treat State and co-wakers at
the Bandit’s own wake; any later facts gained through card movement or public discussion. A claim
about anything beyond those facts is an inference or bluff, not a mechanically certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. If the Raider’s current card changes, the Raider remains the historical
thief and remains the primary hostile asset. If a Business-side card lands on the Raider, that card
does not erase the theft. If another player receives a Bandit card, current-card allegiance may
change, but it does not transfer Raider status.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Bandit is the Cheese Thief itself: a thief is forced to act
at a privately rolled time, making the crime part of the evidence timeline rather than a free
action. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours because several roles wake
more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a generic chance check; it
locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in plain language after Hour
12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Bandit, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of
its own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what
changed when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Bandit can ignore the Burrow Clock
entirely and still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Bandit is the Impostor principle that hostile play is strongest
when it imitates ordinary behavior and turns truthful observations into misleading narratives rather
than inventing impossible facts. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in isolation. A
sighting becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role exceptions,
and what people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture with hours
instead of rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Bandit. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Bandit
should still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Bandit should still be capable of excellent play
by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Bandit's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: own wake hour;
starting Bandit roster; Raider identity; Treat State and co-wakers at the Bandit’s own wake; any
later facts gained through card movement or public discussion. Everything else belongs to one of
four lower-confidence categories: corroborated claims from another player, uncorroborated claims,
deductions from timing, or social reads. The dossier uses these categories because players and bots
must not flatten them into a single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Bandit is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say
'at my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the
second statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Bandit stronger without
increasing its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Bandit's defining balance levers: 1 d12. A wake is not merely a turn to press a
power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player should notice Treat State before action, co-
wakers, whether the theft changes state during the window, and whether the role's own effect occurs
before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Bandit, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Bandit and every other role. At the
beginning of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that
window, authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules
should never turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Bandit's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Bandit, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Bandit, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The useful
questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Bandit is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Bandit's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player entitled
to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to win?
Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Bandit: Do not rush to invent a spectacular story. Establish a plausible
hour and Treat State, listen to the public bracket around the theft, then choose the smallest lie
needed to move the Raider outside that bracket. The goal of the first statement is not to win the
argument immediately. It is to put falsifiable structure on the table. Among Us discussions are
strongest when players report observable sequence before jumping to a suspect; the same principle
applies here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Bandit should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Bandit story can involve wake hour, Treat State, co-wakers, an ordinary Business
claim, or a carefully chosen special-role claim when plausible; the most durable cover keeps true
chronology and changes only the incriminating implication. The dossier does not prohibit lying;
deception is part of the game. What it prohibits is the software accidentally certifying a lie or
exposing hidden information through button availability, timing, bot phrasing, or reconnect
behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Bandit impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Bandit should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker
story, and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Bandit should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact Burrow
Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who else was
awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are you
describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions without
requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Bandit should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player may
withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates a
known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Bandit is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication.
shared hostile knowledge and the fact that the theft is a historical event that card movement cannot
erase. A lie that preserves real wake timing is harder to disprove than a lie that requires multiple
players not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Bandit, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail clears
the whole speaker. For hostile use of Bandit, do not assume one lie requires every other sentence to
be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Bandit, the practical
objective is: keep the historical Raider out of every top eligible Paw Point position, including
ties; sacrificing a non-Raider Bandit, converted Trouble, or converted Hunter is acceptable if it
protects the Raider.

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Bandit must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Bandit player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Bandit, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Bandit is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on Starting
Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Bandit, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For
Bandit, this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting
identities and weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Bandit should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Bandit,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Bandit players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Bandit must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-
set recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not
guarantee it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Bandit, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Bandit operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Bandit, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Bandit bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: protect the historical Raider, avoid
voting known allies unless sacrificial distancing materially reduces Raider risk, and prefer frame
targets whose public timing already contains contradictions.

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Bandit, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the
same wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An
opaque omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Bandit only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when called;
observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw Point once;
Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception: Starting
Bandits know the starting Bandit roster. The earliest starting-Bandit wake becomes the historical
Raider and must steal the Minnow Treats in that window. Other Bandits are accomplices whose job is
to keep the Raider out of the final caught set.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Bandit is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Bandit's largest expert trap is the Treat State timeline can bracket the raid; a witnessed theft,
Dooker Glimpse, Itchy candidate clue, Business die inspection, or contradiction can narrow the
Raider sharply. The dossier intentionally keeps multiple plausible explanations alive long enough
for discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Bandit should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Bandit, specific balance anchor: shared hostile knowledge and the fact that the theft is a
historical event that card movement cannot erase. Specific failure risk: the Treat State timeline
can bracket the raid; a witnessed theft, Dooker Glimpse, Itchy candidate clue, Business die
inspection, or contradiction can narrow the Raider sharply. Playtests should record both, not just
whether the role's side won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Bandit tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid
window, wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action
when legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and
unconverted branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Bandit also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but
it must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Bandit follows 1 d12.
Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At each earlier wake the treats are PRESENT;
at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes see MISSING. The Bandit player records
only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Bandit player uses this role doctrine: Do not rush to invent a
spectacular story. Establish a plausible hour and Treat State, listen to the public bracket around
the theft, then choose the smallest lie needed to move the Raider outside that bracket. Other
players provide competing claims. One player changes a wake story, another has a claim that
conflicts with a Business inspection, and Trouble reports a blind swap. The table distinguishes
historical facts from dawn state rather than concluding that every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Bandit's
vote goal is: keep the historical Raider out of every top eligible Paw Point position, including
ties; sacrificing a non-Raider Bandit, converted Trouble, or converted Hunter is acceptable if it
protects the Raider. All eligible tied leaders are caught. Secondary bond/mark effects resolve, then
the game checks whether the historical Raider is in the final primary catch set. The replay can
explain every transition without exposing facts that were hidden during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Bandit is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, Starting Bandits know the starting Bandit roster. The earliest starting-Bandit wake
becomes the historical Raider and must steal the Minnow Treats in that window. Other Bandits are
accomplices whose job is to keep the Raider out of the final caught set. Strategically, its
strongest advantage is shared hostile knowledge and the fact that the theft is a historical event
that card movement cannot erase. Its balancing vulnerability is the Treat State timeline can bracket
the raid; a witnessed theft, Dooker Glimpse, Itchy candidate clue, Business die inspection, or
contradiction can narrow the Raider sharply.

From Cheese Thief, Bandit inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Bandit's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Bandit, first list the confirmed
private facts available under the role contract: own wake hour; starting Bandit roster; Raider
identity; Treat State and co-wakers at the Bandit’s own wake; any later facts gained through card
movement or public discussion. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest
plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may
distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: keep the
historical Raider out of every top eligible Paw Point position, including ties; sacrificing a non-
Raider Bandit, converted Trouble, or converted Hunter is acceptable if it protects the Raider. The
purpose of this exercise is to train the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a
persuasive narrative. A story can be legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit
every known fact. The facilitator should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a
final explanation.
