Analytical drill 1 for Bandit. Build a deterministic night in which one confirmed fact supports two
different explanations. Use the role's core mechanic—Starting Bandits know the starting Bandit
roster. The earliest starting-Bandit wake becomes the historical Raider and must steal the Minnow
Treats in that window. Other Bandits are accomplices whose job is to keep the Raider out of the
final caught set.—and combine it with exactly one of these uncertainties: card movement, Hunter
conversion, Trouble conversion, Guardian protection, a Snuggle Bond, or a tied Paw Point. Ask
players to write a timeline before naming a suspect. Then ask them to mark every sentence as FACT,
CLAIM, or INFERENCE. The drill passes only if two legal narratives exist before discussion and at
least one additional public statement can distinguish them. This is the intended Ferret Frenzy
texture: uncertainty constrained by rules rather than randomness without structure.


ROLE DEEP DIVE: DOOKER
======================

Role charter. Dooker is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: Business side unless current-card/allegiance rules later change it.
Wake structure: 3 distinct d12s. d6 use: no routine d6. The solidification pass does not replace
this role with an Among Us or Cheese Thief role; it uses those games as design references for how
evidence, deception, conversion, and voting should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: Dooker wakes
three times. First chronological wake is observation. Second chronological wake grants one Glimpse
of a chosen player’s current allegiance as Bandit-aligned or Not Bandit-aligned. Third chronological
wake lets Dooker view one Hammock card and optionally Trip it onto one player, exchanging that
player’s Current Card without seeing the displaced card.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Dooker rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Dooker, the core action
contract is: one binary Glimpse and one informed Hammock Trip; the Trip is informed about the
Hammock card but deliberately uninformed about the player card displaced

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Dooker, confirmed private
facts include three wake windows, three Treat States/co-waker sets, one binary allegiance result at
a specific moment, one known Hammock card, and exact knowledge of whether/where the viewed Hammock
card was moved. A claim about anything beyond those facts is an inference or bluff, not a
mechanically certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. Glimpse reads allegiance at the moment of the Glimpse, after any conversion
triggers already resolved in that hour. The result can become stale after later card movement. Trip
never changes Starting Role or historical Raider status.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Dooker is the repeated value of wake collisions and lone
observations: Dooker samples more of the private night than a normal ferret but never sees the whole
timeline. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours because several roles wake
more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a generic chance check; it
locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in plain language after Hour
12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Dooker, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of
its own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what
changed when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Dooker can ignore the Burrow Clock
entirely and still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Dooker is modern role design that reinterprets evidence: a strong
ability supplies information but does not replace the need to reconcile routes, identities, and
later state changes. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in isolation. A sighting
becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role exceptions, and what
people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture with hours instead of
rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Dooker. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Dooker
should still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Dooker should still be capable of excellent play
by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Dooker's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: three wake windows,
three Treat States/co-waker sets, one binary allegiance result at a specific moment, one known
Hammock card, and exact knowledge of whether/where the viewed Hammock card was moved. Everything
else belongs to one of four lower-confidence categories: corroborated claims from another player,
uncorroborated claims, deductions from timing, or social reads. The dossier uses these categories
because players and bots must not flatten them into a single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Dooker is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say
'at my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the
second statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Dooker stronger without
increasing its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Dooker's defining balance levers: 3 distinct d12s. A wake is not merely a turn
to press a power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player should notice Treat State before
action, co-wakers, whether the theft changes state during the window, and whether the role's own
effect occurs before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Dooker, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Dooker and every other role. At the
beginning of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that
window, authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules
should never turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Dooker's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Dooker, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Dooker, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The useful
questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Dooker is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Dooker's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player entitled
to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to win?
Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Dooker: Lead with chronology before conclusion. State the three wake windows
and Treat States, identify which claims are direct observations, then decide whether exposing the
Glimpse or Trip helps more than it helps a hostile player reconstruct your information. The goal of
the first statement is not to win the argument immediately. It is to put falsifiable structure on
the table. Among Us discussions are strongest when players report observable sequence before jumping
to a suspect; the same principle applies here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Dooker should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Dooker story can involve can reveal all three hours or stagger information, can
disclose Glimpse without immediately revealing which wake produced it, and can truthfully describe
the viewed Hammock card while remaining uncertain about the displaced card. The dossier does not
prohibit lying; deception is part of the game. What it prohibits is the software accidentally
certifying a lie or exposing hidden information through button availability, timing, bot phrasing,
or reconnect behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Dooker impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Dooker should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker
story, and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Dooker should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact Burrow
Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who else was
awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are you
describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions without
requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Dooker should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player may
withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates a
known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Dooker is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication.
broad temporal coverage plus one hard-but-binary scan and one controlled source of card-state
uncertainty. A lie that preserves real wake timing is harder to disprove than a lie that requires
multiple players not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Dooker, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail clears
the whole speaker. For hostile use of Dooker, do not assume one lie requires every other sentence to
be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Dooker, the practical
objective is: vote for the player whose total story best fits the Raider timeline after accounting
for stale Glimpse information and card movement; do not mechanically vote every Bandit-aligned
Glimpse target if the historical Raider evidence points elsewhere.

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Dooker must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Dooker player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Dooker, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Dooker is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on Starting
Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Dooker, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For
Dooker, this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting
identities and weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Dooker should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Dooker,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Dooker players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Dooker must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-
set recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not
guarantee it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Dooker, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Dooker operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Dooker, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Dooker bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: separate historical theft suspicion from
current-allegiance suspicion, preserve timestamps on Glimpse/Trip, and never invent knowledge of the
card displaced by Trip.

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Dooker, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the
same wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An
opaque omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Dooker only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when called;
observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw Point once;
Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception: Dooker wakes
three times. First chronological wake is observation. Second chronological wake grants one Glimpse
of a chosen player’s current allegiance as Bandit-aligned or Not Bandit-aligned. Third chronological
wake lets Dooker view one Hammock card and optionally Trip it onto one player, exchanging that
player’s Current Card without seeing the displaced card.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Dooker is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Dooker's largest expert trap is three wakes make Dooker easier to cross-check; the Trip can
accidentally help Bandits, can make Dooker’s earlier Glimpse stale, and can create false confidence
if players conflate current allegiance with historical Raider identity. The dossier intentionally
keeps multiple plausible explanations alive long enough for discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Dooker should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Dooker, specific balance anchor: broad temporal coverage plus one hard-but-binary scan and one
controlled source of card-state uncertainty. Specific failure risk: three wakes make Dooker easier
to cross-check; the Trip can accidentally help Bandits, can make Dooker’s earlier Glimpse stale, and
can create false confidence if players conflate current allegiance with historical Raider identity.
Playtests should record both, not just whether the role's side won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Dooker tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid
window, wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action
when legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and
unconverted branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Dooker also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but
it must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Dooker follows 3
distinct d12s. Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At each earlier wake the treats
are PRESENT; at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes see MISSING. The Dooker
player records only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Dooker player uses this role doctrine: Lead with chronology before
conclusion. State the three wake windows and Treat States, identify which claims are direct
observations, then decide whether exposing the Glimpse or Trip helps more than it helps a hostile
player reconstruct your information. Other players provide competing claims. One player changes a
wake story, another has a claim that conflicts with a Business inspection, and Trouble reports a
blind swap. The table distinguishes historical facts from dawn state rather than concluding that
every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Dooker's
vote goal is: vote for the player whose total story best fits the Raider timeline after accounting
for stale Glimpse information and card movement; do not mechanically vote every Bandit-aligned
Glimpse target if the historical Raider evidence points elsewhere. All eligible tied leaders are
caught. Secondary bond/mark effects resolve, then the game checks whether the historical Raider is
in the final primary catch set. The replay can explain every transition without exposing facts that
were hidden during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Dooker is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, Dooker wakes three times. First chronological wake is observation. Second
chronological wake grants one Glimpse of a chosen player’s current allegiance as Bandit-aligned or
Not Bandit-aligned. Third chronological wake lets Dooker view one Hammock card and optionally Trip
it onto one player, exchanging that player’s Current Card without seeing the displaced card.
Strategically, its strongest advantage is broad temporal coverage plus one hard-but-binary scan and
one controlled source of card-state uncertainty. Its balancing vulnerability is three wakes make
Dooker easier to cross-check; the Trip can accidentally help Bandits, can make Dooker’s earlier
Glimpse stale, and can create false confidence if players conflate current allegiance with
historical Raider identity.

From Cheese Thief, Dooker inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Dooker's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Dooker, first list the confirmed
private facts available under the role contract: three wake windows, three Treat States/co-waker
sets, one binary allegiance result at a specific moment, one known Hammock card, and exact knowledge
of whether/where the viewed Hammock card was moved. Next list the strongest truthful public claim,
the strongest plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can
ask that may distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's
objective: vote for the player whose total story best fits the Raider timeline after accounting for
stale Glimpse information and card movement; do not mechanically vote every Bandit-aligned Glimpse
target if the historical Raider evidence points elsewhere. The purpose of this exercise is to train
the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be
legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator
should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 2: late raid. Raider acts in Hours 10–12, so many players see PRESENT and late
witnesses become disproportionately important. For Dooker, first list the confirmed private facts
available under the role contract: three wake windows, three Treat States/co-waker sets, one binary
allegiance result at a specific moment, one known Hammock card, and exact knowledge of whether/where
the viewed Hammock card was moved. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest
plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may

historical Raider evidence points elsewhere. The purpose of this exercise is to train the
distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be
legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator
should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 3: crowded theft window. three or more players share the theft hour, creating direct

Practice case 6: Dooker Trip after an earlier scan. a known Hammock card moves after information was

Practice case 7: converted Hunter in trusted pair. Guardian has a true starting-role recognition

Practice case 8: Guardian redirect vote. the apparent top suspect is protected, so the next-highest
eligible total becomes the actual capture target. For Dooker, first list the confirmed private facts
available under the role contract: three wake windows, three Treat States/co-waker sets, one binary
allegiance result at a specific moment, one known Hammock card, and exact knowledge of whether/where
the viewed Hammock card was moved. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest
plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may
distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: vote for
the player whose total story best fits the Raider timeline after accounting for stale Glimpse
information and card movement; do not mechanically vote every Bandit-aligned Glimpse target if the
historical Raider evidence points elsewhere. The purpose of this exercise is to train the
distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be
legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator
should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 9: Snuggle Bond exposure. a caught bonded player causes the partner to be revealed in
results, adding information without rewriting the historical theft. For Dooker, first list the
confirmed private facts available under the role contract: three wake windows, three Treat
States/co-waker sets, one binary allegiance result at a specific moment, one known Hammock card, and
exact knowledge of whether/where the viewed Hammock card was moved. Next list the strongest truthful
public claim, the strongest plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question
another player can ask that may distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using
this role's objective: vote for the player whose total story best fits the Raider timeline after
accounting for stale Glimpse information and card movement; do not mechanically vote every Bandit-
aligned Glimpse target if the historical Raider evidence points elsewhere. The purpose of this
exercise is to train the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive
narrative. A story can be legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every
known fact. The facilitator should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final
explanation.

Practice case 10: tie at Paw Point. two or more unprotected eligible players share the highest total
and are caught together, creating a controlled multi-suspect resolution. For Dooker, first list the
confirmed private facts available under the role contract: three wake windows, three Treat
States/co-waker sets, one binary allegiance result at a specific moment, one known Hammock card, and
exact knowledge of whether/where the viewed Hammock card was moved. Next list the strongest truthful
public claim, the strongest plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question
another player can ask that may distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using
this role's objective: vote for the player whose total story best fits the Raider timeline after
accounting for stale Glimpse information and card movement; do not mechanically vote every Bandit-
aligned Glimpse target if the historical Raider evidence points elsewhere. The purpose of this
exercise is to train the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive
narrative. A story can be legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every
known fact. The facilitator should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final
explanation.


ROLE DEEP DIVE: ITCHY
=====================

Role charter. Itchy is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: Business side unless current-card/allegiance rules later change it.
Wake structure: 1 d12 normally; 2 distinct d12s on activation. d6 use: setup d6; 5–6 activates the
observation night, 1–4 does not. The solidification pass does not replace this role with an Among Us
or Cheese Thief role; it uses those games as design references for how evidence, deception,
conversion, and voting should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: Itchy always receives normal wake
evidence. On d6 5–6, Itchy gains a second distinct wake and, after the raid is committed, receives a
partial two-player clue containing the historical Raider and one valid non-Raider decoy whenever the
game can construct such a pair.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Itchy rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Itchy, the core action
contract is: conditional extra observation plus a two-candidate Raider clue; never an exact
identification

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Itchy, confirmed private
facts include activation result; one or two wake windows; Treat State/co-wakers; if activated,
exactly two clue candidates with the guarantee that one is the historical Raider when a valid decoy
exists. A claim about anything beyond those facts is an inference or bluff, not a mechanically
certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. The decoy must be a real active non-Raider player, not a Hammock card. If no
legal decoy exists, the app must explicitly mark the clue as degraded rather than silently
presenting a one-name “pair.” Card movement after the raid does not change which player was the
historical Raider in the clue.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Itchy is direct witness information that can be useful
without being a complete verdict; the design keeps a short list of plausible thieves rather than
simply printing the answer. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours because
several roles wake more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a generic
chance check; it locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in plain
language after Hour 12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Itchy, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of its
own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what changed
when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Itchy can ignore the Burrow Clock entirely and
still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Itchy is the principle that strong evidence should remain
interpretable: like identity-contaminating roles, Itchy makes players ask what a clue proves and
what it does not prove. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in isolation. A sighting
becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role exceptions, and what
people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture with hours instead of
rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Itchy. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Itchy should
still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Itchy should still be capable of excellent play
by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Itchy's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: activation result; one
or two wake windows; Treat State/co-wakers; if activated, exactly two clue candidates with the
guarantee that one is the historical Raider when a valid decoy exists. Everything else belongs to
one of four lower-confidence categories: corroborated claims from another player, uncorroborated
claims, deductions from timing, or social reads. The dossier uses these categories because players
and bots must not flatten them into a single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Itchy is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say 'at
my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the second
statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Itchy stronger without increasing
its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Itchy's defining balance levers: 1 d12 normally; 2 distinct d12s on activation.
A wake is not merely a turn to press a power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player
should notice Treat State before action, co-wakers, whether the theft changes state during the
window, and whether the role's own effect occurs before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Itchy, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Itchy and every other role. At the beginning
of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that window,
authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules should never
turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Itchy's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Itchy, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Itchy, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The useful
questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Itchy is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Itchy's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player entitled
to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to win?
Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Itchy: If activated, give the timeline first and the candidate pair second.
This lets the table compare the pair against Treat State and co-waker claims rather than treating
the pair as a command to vote randomly between two names. The goal of the first statement is not to
win the argument immediately. It is to put falsifiable structure on the table. Among Us discussions
are strongest when players report observable sequence before jumping to a suspect; the same
principle applies here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Itchy should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Itchy story can involve can truthfully report no activation, can reveal one or
both wake windows, and can reveal candidate pair without claiming certainty about which candidate is
Raider. The dossier does not prohibit lying; deception is part of the game. What it prohibits is the
software accidentally certifying a lie or exposing hidden information through button availability,
timing, bot phrasing, or reconnect behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Itchy impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Itchy should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker story,
and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Itchy should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact Burrow
Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who else was
awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are you
describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions without
requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Itchy should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player may
withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates a
known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Itchy is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication. on
activated nights, Itchy converts a broad mystery into a constrained suspect pair while still
requiring social deduction. A lie that preserves real wake timing is harder to disprove than a lie
that requires multiple players not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Itchy, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail clears
the whole speaker. For hostile use of Itchy, do not assume one lie requires every other sentence to
be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Itchy, the practical
objective is: use the clue to constrain rather than dictate. Prefer the candidate whose wake story
conflicts with the public theft bracket, but remain willing to vote outside the pair only if a rules
edge case invalidated the normal guarantee.

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Itchy must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Itchy player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Itchy, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Itchy is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on Starting
Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Itchy, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For Itchy,
this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting identities and
weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Itchy should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Itchy,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Itchy players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Itchy must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-set
recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not guarantee
it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Itchy, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Itchy operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Itchy, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Itchy bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: never turn a two-candidate clue into an
exact accusation; record which evidence is ordinary wake evidence and which evidence comes from
activation.

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Itchy, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the same
wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An opaque
omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Itchy only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when called;
observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw Point once;
Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception: Itchy always
receives normal wake evidence. On d6 5–6, Itchy gains a second distinct wake and, after the raid is
committed, receives a partial two-player clue containing the historical Raider and one valid non-
Raider decoy whenever the game can construct such a pair.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Itchy is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Itchy's largest expert trap is activation is uncommon, candidate evidence can be framed, and Itchy
becomes an attractive Bandit target for contradiction or false counterclaims because the clue is
powerful but not self-verifying. The dossier intentionally keeps multiple plausible explanations
alive long enough for discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Itchy should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Itchy, specific balance anchor: on activated nights, Itchy converts a broad mystery into a
constrained suspect pair while still requiring social deduction. Specific failure risk: activation
is uncommon, candidate evidence can be framed, and Itchy becomes an attractive Bandit target for
contradiction or false counterclaims because the clue is powerful but not self-verifying. Playtests
should record both, not just whether the role's side won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Itchy tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid window,
wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action when
legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and unconverted
branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Itchy also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but it
must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Itchy follows 1 d12
normally; 2 distinct d12s on activation. Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At
each earlier wake the treats are PRESENT; at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes
see MISSING. The Itchy player records only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Itchy player uses this role doctrine: If activated, give the timeline
first and the candidate pair second. This lets the table compare the pair against Treat State and
co-waker claims rather than treating the pair as a command to vote randomly between two names. Other
players provide competing claims. One player changes a wake story, another has a claim that
conflicts with a Business inspection, and Trouble reports a blind swap. The table distinguishes
historical facts from dawn state rather than concluding that every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Itchy's
vote goal is: use the clue to constrain rather than dictate. Prefer the candidate whose wake story
conflicts with the public theft bracket, but remain willing to vote outside the pair only if a rules
edge case invalidated the normal guarantee. All eligible tied leaders are caught. Secondary
bond/mark effects resolve, then the game checks whether the historical Raider is in the final
primary catch set. The replay can explain every transition without exposing facts that were hidden
during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Itchy is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, Itchy always receives normal wake evidence. On d6 5–6, Itchy gains a second distinct
wake and, after the raid is committed, receives a partial two-player clue containing the historical
Raider and one valid non-Raider decoy whenever the game can construct such a pair. Strategically,
its strongest advantage is on activated nights, Itchy converts a broad mystery into a constrained
suspect pair while still requiring social deduction. Its balancing vulnerability is activation is
uncommon, candidate evidence can be framed, and Itchy becomes an attractive Bandit target for
contradiction or false counterclaims because the clue is powerful but not self-verifying.

From Cheese Thief, Itchy inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Itchy's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Itchy, first list the confirmed private
facts available under the role contract: activation result; one or two wake windows; Treat State/co-
wakers; if activated, exactly two clue candidates with the guarantee that one is the historical
Raider when a valid decoy exists. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest
plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may
distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: use the
clue to constrain rather than dictate. Prefer the candidate whose wake story conflicts with the
public theft bracket, but remain willing to vote outside the pair only if a rules edge case
invalidated the normal guarantee. The purpose of this exercise is to train the distinction between a
mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be legal yet badly
supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator should not reveal
hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 2: late raid. Raider acts in Hours 10–12, so many players see PRESENT and late
