ROLE DEEP DIVE: TROUBLE
=======================

Role charter. Trouble is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: starts Business side and may become a Bandit-side accomplice. Wake
structure: 1 d12. d6 use: no routine d6. The solidification pass does not replace this role with an
Among Us or Cheese Thief role; it uses those games as design references for how evidence, deception,
conversion, and voting should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: At Trouble’s wake, Trouble
blindly swaps the Current Cards of two distinct other players. If Trouble is awake in the exact
theft window, Trouble becomes In On It before the swap resolves and receives the historical Raider
identity as part of that conversion.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Trouble rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Trouble, the core action
contract is: one blind two-player Current Card swap, always based on Starting Role authority even if
Trouble’s own Current Card changed earlier

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Trouble, confirmed private
facts include own wake evidence; exact identities of the two positions swapped; no knowledge of
either swapped card; if converted, the fact of conversion and historical Raider identity. A claim
about anything beyond those facts is an inference or bluff, not a mechanically certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. Trouble cannot target self and cannot select the same player twice.
Conversion occurs before the swap in the theft window. The player-bound accomplice status survives
later card movement.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Trouble is Follower conversion tied to witnessing the crime.
The key lesson is that loyalty can change because of a night event rather than because an accomplice
was preassigned at setup.. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours because
several roles wake more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a generic
chance check; it locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in plain
language after Hour 12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Trouble, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of
its own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what
changed when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Trouble can ignore the Burrow Clock
entirely and still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Trouble is evidence contamination: role/state movement means a
statement that was true earlier can be misleading later, much as modern roles can invalidate
formerly conclusive identity evidence. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in
isolation. A sighting becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role
exceptions, and what people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture
with hours instead of rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Trouble. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Trouble
should still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Trouble should still be capable of excellent
play by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Trouble's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: own wake evidence;
exact identities of the two positions swapped; no knowledge of either swapped card; if converted,
the fact of conversion and historical Raider identity. Everything else belongs to one of four lower-
confidence categories: corroborated claims from another player, uncorroborated claims, deductions
from timing, or social reads. The dossier uses these categories because players and bots must not
flatten them into a single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Trouble is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say
'at my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the
second statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Trouble stronger without
increasing its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Trouble's defining balance levers: 1 d12. A wake is not merely a turn to press
a power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player should notice Treat State before action,
co-wakers, whether the theft changes state during the window, and whether the role's own effect
occurs before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Trouble, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Trouble and every other role. At the
beginning of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that
window, authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules
should never turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Trouble's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Trouble, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Trouble, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The
useful questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Trouble is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Trouble's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player
entitled to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to
win? Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Trouble: A loyal Trouble should usually disclose the two swapped positions
early enough that the group does not treat post-swap role contradictions as automatic lies. A
converted Trouble should decide whether truthful disclosure creates more useful confusion than
concealment. The goal of the first statement is not to win the argument immediately. It is to put
falsifiable structure on the table. Among Us discussions are strongest when players report
observable sequence before jumping to a suspect; the same principle applies here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Trouble should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Trouble story can involve can truthfully reveal the swap while withholding
allegiance change; a loyal Trouble can use swap facts to explain why role claims may no longer match
Current Cards; a converted Trouble can exploit the same uncertainty to shield the Raider. The
dossier does not prohibit lying; deception is part of the game. What it prohibits is the software
accidentally certifying a lie or exposing hidden information through button availability, timing,
bot phrasing, or reconnect behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Trouble impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Trouble should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker
story, and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Trouble should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact Burrow
Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who else was
awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are you
describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions without
requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Trouble should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player
may withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates
a known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Trouble is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication.
creates legitimate card-state ambiguity and, on theft-window collision, can become a thematically
clean Follower-like accomplice without adding a ninth role. A lie that preserves real wake timing is
harder to disprove than a lie that requires multiple players not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Trouble, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail clears
the whole speaker. For hostile use of Trouble, do not assume one lie requires every other sentence
to be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Trouble, the practical
objective is: loyal Trouble votes for the historical Raider; converted Trouble protects the
historical Raider even if that means accepting personal suspicion. Trouble’s own capture never
substitutes for capturing the Raider..

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Trouble must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Trouble player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Trouble, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Trouble is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on Starting
Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Trouble, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For
Trouble, this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting
identities and weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Trouble should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Trouble,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Trouble players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Trouble must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-
set recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not
guarantee it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Trouble, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Trouble operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Trouble, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Trouble bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: maintain a strict distinction between
swap-position knowledge and card-identity ignorance. If converted, the bot may coordinate
strategically from known Raider identity but cannot fabricate having seen the swapped cards..

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Trouble, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the
same wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An
opaque omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Trouble only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when
called; observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw
Point once; Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception:
At Trouble’s wake, Trouble blindly swaps the Current Cards of two distinct other players. If Trouble
is awake in the exact theft window, Trouble becomes In On It before the swap resolves and receives
the historical Raider identity as part of that conversion.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Trouble is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Trouble's largest expert trap is blindness is real; Trouble can accidentally move a Bandit card,
disrupt a useful Business role, or produce a swap that later makes Trouble look responsible for
contradictions it could not predict. The dossier intentionally keeps multiple plausible explanations
alive long enough for discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Trouble should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Trouble, specific balance anchor: creates legitimate card-state ambiguity and, on theft-window
collision, can become a thematically clean Follower-like accomplice without adding a ninth role.
Specific failure risk: blindness is real; Trouble can accidentally move a Bandit card, disrupt a
useful Business role, or produce a swap that later makes Trouble look responsible for contradictions
it could not predict. Playtests should record both, not just whether the role's side won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Trouble tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid
window, wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action
when legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and
unconverted branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Trouble also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but
it must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Trouble follows 1
d12. Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At each earlier wake the treats are
PRESENT; at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes see MISSING. The Trouble player
records only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Trouble player uses this role doctrine: A loyal Trouble should usually
disclose the two swapped positions early enough that the group does not treat post-swap role
contradictions as automatic lies. A converted Trouble should decide whether truthful disclosure
creates more useful confusion than concealment. Other players provide competing claims. One player
changes a wake story, another has a claim that conflicts with a Business inspection, and Trouble
reports a blind swap. The table distinguishes historical facts from dawn state rather than
concluding that every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Trouble's
vote goal is: loyal Trouble votes for the historical Raider; converted Trouble protects the
historical Raider even if that means accepting personal suspicion. Trouble’s own capture never
substitutes for capturing the Raider.. All eligible tied leaders are caught. Secondary bond/mark
effects resolve, then the game checks whether the historical Raider is in the final primary catch
set. The replay can explain every transition without exposing facts that were hidden during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Trouble is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, At Trouble’s wake, Trouble blindly swaps the Current Cards of two distinct other
players. If Trouble is awake in the exact theft window, Trouble becomes In On It before the swap
resolves and receives the historical Raider identity as part of that conversion. Strategically, its
strongest advantage is creates legitimate card-state ambiguity and, on theft-window collision, can
become a thematically clean Follower-like accomplice without adding a ninth role. Its balancing
vulnerability is blindness is real; Trouble can accidentally move a Bandit card, disrupt a useful
Business role, or produce a swap that later makes Trouble look responsible for contradictions it
could not predict.

From Cheese Thief, Trouble inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Trouble's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Trouble, first list the confirmed
private facts available under the role contract: own wake evidence; exact identities of the two
positions swapped; no knowledge of either swapped card; if converted, the fact of conversion and
historical Raider identity. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest plausible
hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may distinguish
them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: loyal Trouble votes
for the historical Raider; converted Trouble protects the historical Raider even if that means
accepting personal suspicion. Trouble’s own capture never substitutes for capturing the Raider.. The
purpose of this exercise is to train the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a
persuasive narrative. A story can be legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit
every known fact. The facilitator should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a
final explanation.

Practice case 2: late raid. Raider acts in Hours 10–12, so many players see PRESENT and late

Analytical drill 1 for Trouble. Build a deterministic night in which one confirmed fact supports two
different explanations. Use the role's core mechanic—At Trouble’s wake, Trouble blindly swaps the
Current Cards of two distinct other players. If Trouble is awake in the exact theft window, Trouble
becomes In On It before the swap resolves and receives the historical Raider identity as part of
that conversion.—and combine it with exactly one of these uncertainties: card movement, Hunter
conversion, Trouble conversion, Guardian protection, a Snuggle Bond, or a tied Paw Point. Ask
players to write a timeline before naming a suspect. Then ask them to mark every sentence as FACT,
CLAIM, or INFERENCE. The drill passes only if two legal narratives exist before discussion and at
least one additional public statement can distinguish them. This is the intended Ferret Frenzy
texture: uncertainty constrained by rules rather than randomness without structure.


ROLE DEEP DIVE: BUSINESS
========================

Role charter. Business is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: Business side unless current-card/allegiance rules later change it.
Wake structure: 1 d12. d6 use: no routine d6. The solidification pass does not replace this role
with an Among Us or Cheese Thief role; it uses those games as design references for how evidence,
deception, conversion, and voting should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: Business is the
ordinary evidence role. It sees Treat State and co-wakers at its single wake. If it wakes alone, it
may inspect exactly one wake result belonging to one chosen other player; for a multi-wake target,
the app reveals one randomly selected result and does not reveal how many additional wake results
the target has.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Business rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Business, the core action
contract is: normally no special action; lone wake unlocks one limited wake-hour inspection

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Business, confirmed private
facts include own wake hour; Treat State; co-wakers; when alone, one inspected wake result from a
chosen player; no direct role scan and no allegiance scan. A claim about anything beyond those facts
is an inference or bluff, not a mechanically certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. Lone means no other player was scheduled in the same wake window when
observation begins. A hidden Raider action at that same hour still means the Raider is a co-waker,
so Business is not lone. Inspection never reveals a role name or whether the inspected result is the
target’s only wake.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Business is the Sleepyhead model and lone-wake die peek are
the clearest direct influence: ordinary players receive useful mechanical evidence without needing a
unique power card. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours because several
roles wake more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a generic chance
check; it locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in plain language
after Hour 12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Business, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of
its own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what
changed when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Business can ignore the Burrow Clock
entirely and still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Business is Crew-style strength comes from aggregation. One
ordinary player rarely solves the round, but multiple consistent observations create a
route/timeline that hostile claims must fit.. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in
isolation. A sighting becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role
exceptions, and what people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture
with hours instead of rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Business. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Business
should still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Business should still be capable of excellent
play by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Business's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: own wake hour; Treat
State; co-wakers; when alone, one inspected wake result from a chosen player; no direct role scan
and no allegiance scan. Everything else belongs to one of four lower-confidence categories:
corroborated claims from another player, uncorroborated claims, deductions from timing, or social
reads. The dossier uses these categories because players and bots must not flatten them into a
single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Business is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say
'at my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the
second statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Business stronger without
increasing its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Business's defining balance levers: 1 d12. A wake is not merely a turn to press
a power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player should notice Treat State before action,
co-wakers, whether the theft changes state during the window, and whether the role's own effect
occurs before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Business, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Business and every other role. At the
beginning of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that
window, authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules
should never turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Business's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Business, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Business, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The
useful questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Business is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Business's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player
entitled to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to
win? Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Business: State the hour and Treat State first. If alone, disclose the
inspected result only when it meaningfully tests someone’s story; otherwise hold it briefly so a
claimant cannot simply mirror the number you already announced. The goal of the first statement is
not to win the argument immediately. It is to put falsifiable structure on the table. Among Us
discussions are strongest when players report observable sequence before jumping to a suspect; the
same principle applies here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Business should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Business story can involve simple claims about hour, Treat State, co-wakers, and
optional inspected result. Because Bandits can imitate this structure, credibility must come from
consistency and corroboration rather than uniqueness. The dossier does not prohibit lying; deception
is part of the game. What it prohibits is the software accidentally certifying a lie or exposing
hidden information through button availability, timing, bot phrasing, or reconnect behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Business impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Business should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker
story, and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Business should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact
Burrow Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who
else was awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are
you describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions
without requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Business should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player
may withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates
a known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Business is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication.
forms the baseline evidence network against which special-role claims and hostile stories can be
tested. A lie that preserves real wake timing is harder to disprove than a lie that requires
multiple players not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Business, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail
clears the whole speaker. For hostile use of Business, do not assume one lie requires every other
sentence to be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Business, the practical
objective is: vote from the reconstructed theft bracket and contradiction network, not from role
glamour. A plain Business claim that fits two other observations can be stronger than a flashy
special-role claim that cannot be reconciled..

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Business must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Business player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Business, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Business is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on
Starting Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Business, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For
Business, this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting
identities and weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Business should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Business,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Business players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Business must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-
set recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not
guarantee it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Business, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Business operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Business, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Business bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: prioritize timeline consistency, ask
direct questions about wake hours and Treat State, and treat inspected multi-wake results as partial
rather than exhaustive.

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Business, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the
same wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An
opaque omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Business only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when
called; observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw
Point once; Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception:
Business is the ordinary evidence role. It sees Treat State and co-wakers at its single wake. If it
wakes alone, it may inspect exactly one wake result belonging to one chosen other player; for a
multi-wake target, the app reveals one randomly selected result and does not reveal how many
additional wake results the target has.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Business is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Business's largest expert trap is has little privileged power, can be impersonated easily, and may
overvalue a single inspected die when the target is a multi-wake role. The dossier intentionally
keeps multiple plausible explanations alive long enough for discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Business should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Business, specific balance anchor: forms the baseline evidence network against which special-
role claims and hostile stories can be tested. Specific failure risk: has little privileged power,
can be impersonated easily, and may overvalue a single inspected die when the target is a multi-wake
role. Playtests should record both, not just whether the role's side won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Business tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid
window, wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action
when legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and
unconverted branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Business also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but
it must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Business follows 1
d12. Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At each earlier wake the treats are
PRESENT; at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes see MISSING. The Business player
records only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Business player uses this role doctrine: State the hour and Treat State
first. If alone, disclose the inspected result only when it meaningfully tests someone’s story;
otherwise hold it briefly so a claimant cannot simply mirror the number you already announced. Other
players provide competing claims. One player changes a wake story, another has a claim that
conflicts with a Business inspection, and Trouble reports a blind swap. The table distinguishes
historical facts from dawn state rather than concluding that every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Business's
vote goal is: vote from the reconstructed theft bracket and contradiction network, not from role
glamour. A plain Business claim that fits two other observations can be stronger than a flashy
special-role claim that cannot be reconciled.. All eligible tied leaders are caught. Secondary
bond/mark effects resolve, then the game checks whether the historical Raider is in the final
primary catch set. The replay can explain every transition without exposing facts that were hidden
during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Business is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, Business is the ordinary evidence role. It sees Treat State and co-wakers at its
single wake. If it wakes alone, it may inspect exactly one wake result belonging to one chosen other
player; for a multi-wake target, the app reveals one randomly selected result and does not reveal
how many additional wake results the target has. Strategically, its strongest advantage is forms the
baseline evidence network against which special-role claims and hostile stories can be tested. Its
balancing vulnerability is has little privileged power, can be impersonated easily, and may
overvalue a single inspected die when the target is a multi-wake role.

From Cheese Thief, Business inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Business's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Business, first list the confirmed
private facts available under the role contract: own wake hour; Treat State; co-wakers; when alone,
one inspected wake result from a chosen player; no direct role scan and no allegiance scan. Next
list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest plausible hostile reinterpretation of that
claim, and one question another player can ask that may distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw
Point consequences using this role's objective: vote from the reconstructed theft bracket and
contradiction network, not from role glamour. A plain Business claim that fits two other
observations can be stronger than a flashy special-role claim that cannot be reconciled.. The
purpose of this exercise is to train the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a
persuasive narrative. A story can be legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit
every known fact. The facilitator should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a
final explanation.
