Analytical drill 1 for Business. Build a deterministic night in which one confirmed fact supports
two different explanations. Use the role's core mechanic—Business is the ordinary evidence role. It
sees Treat State and co-wakers at its single wake. If it wakes alone, it may inspect exactly one
wake result belonging to one chosen other player; for a multi-wake target, the app reveals one
randomly selected result and does not reveal how many additional wake results the target has.—and
combine it with exactly one of these uncertainties: card movement, Hunter conversion, Trouble
conversion, Guardian protection, a Snuggle Bond, or a tied Paw Point. Ask players to write a
timeline before naming a suspect. Then ask them to mark every sentence as FACT, CLAIM, or INFERENCE.
The drill passes only if two legal narratives exist before discussion and at least one additional
public statement can distinguish them. This is the intended Ferret Frenzy texture: uncertainty
constrained by rules rather than randomness without structure.


ROLE DEEP DIVE: SNUGGLER
========================

Role charter. Snuggler is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: retains normal allegiance; the bond is not a third faction. Wake
structure: 1 d12. d6 use: no routine d6. The solidification pass does not replace this role with an
Among Us or Cheese Thief role; it uses those games as design references for how evidence, deception,
conversion, and voting should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: Before Hour 1, Snuggler chooses
two distinct other players to become Snuggle-Bonded. The bonded players learn each other’s identity
as partners but not each other’s roles. Bond status is player-bound. If one bonded player is caught
by vote or a valid Hunt Mark, the other becomes Bond-Revealed in the final resolution; Bond-Reveal
exposes identity/current card for results but is not itself a Paw Point capture.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Snuggler rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Snuggler, the core action
contract is: one pre-night player relationship choice; no extra night action beyond ordinary wake
evidence

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Snuggler, confirmed private
facts include the selected bonded pair; own normal wake evidence; no alignment information about the
pair unless obtained elsewhere. A claim about anything beyond those facts is an inference or bluff,
not a mechanically certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. Snuggler cannot bond self in the solidified base rule. Bond survives card
swaps and allegiance changes. Guardian protection blocks Paw Point capture of a protected bonded
player but does not erase the bond or prevent the partner from being Bond-Revealed if a different
valid trigger occurs.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Snuggler is borrows the social topology lesson from Followers
without copying allegiance: who knows whom and who has reason to coordinate can be as important as
raw role powers. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours because several
roles wake more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a generic chance
check; it locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in plain language
after Hour 12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Snuggler, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of
its own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what
changed when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Snuggler can ignore the Burrow Clock
entirely and still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Snuggler is mirrors the broader lesson that visible cooperation is
ambiguous. Two players moving or voting together may be mutually informed, allied, bonded, or simply
convinced by the same evidence.. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in isolation. A
sighting becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role exceptions,
and what people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture with hours
instead of rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Snuggler. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Snuggler
should still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Snuggler should still be capable of excellent
play by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Snuggler's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: the selected bonded
pair; own normal wake evidence; no alignment information about the pair unless obtained elsewhere.
Everything else belongs to one of four lower-confidence categories: corroborated claims from another
player, uncorroborated claims, deductions from timing, or social reads. The dossier uses these
categories because players and bots must not flatten them into a single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Snuggler is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say
'at my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the
second statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Snuggler stronger without
increasing its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Snuggler's defining balance levers: 1 d12. A wake is not merely a turn to press
a power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player should notice Treat State before action,
co-wakers, whether the theft changes state during the window, and whether the role's own effect
occurs before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Snuggler, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Snuggler and every other role. At the
beginning of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that
window, authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules
should never turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Snuggler's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Snuggler, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Snuggler, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The
useful questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Snuggler is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Snuggler's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player
entitled to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to
win? Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Snuggler: Do not present the bond as a clear. If revealing it, explain
exactly what it proves: two people were linked before the night and know each other as partners. It
says nothing about Raider status or later conversion. The goal of the first statement is not to win
the argument immediately. It is to put falsifiable structure on the table. Among Us discussions are
strongest when players report observable sequence before jumping to a suspect; the same principle
applies here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Snuggler should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Snuggler story can involve can reveal or conceal the pair. Bonded players may
explain cooperation by claiming the bond, but a bond claim never clears either player because one or
both may be hostile. The dossier does not prohibit lying; deception is part of the game. What it
prohibits is the software accidentally certifying a lie or exposing hidden information through
button availability, timing, bot phrasing, or reconnect behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Snuggler impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Snuggler should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker
story, and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Snuggler should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact
Burrow Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who
else was awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are
you describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions
without requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Snuggler should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player
may withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates
a known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Snuggler is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication.
creates a human trust channel that is mechanically real but alignment-neutral, making social
alliances less diagnostic and producing useful ambiguity without adding another hostile faction. A
lie that preserves real wake timing is harder to disprove than a lie that requires multiple players
not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Snuggler, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail
clears the whole speaker. For hostile use of Snuggler, do not assume one lie requires every other
sentence to be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Snuggler, the practical
objective is: vote normally toward the historical Raider. Consider the secondary Bond-Reveal
consequence because catching one bonded player can expose the other, but do not treat exposure as
automatic primary victory unless another rule also catches the Raider..

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Snuggler must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Snuggler player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Snuggler, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Snuggler is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on
Starting Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Snuggler, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For
Snuggler, this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting
identities and weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Snuggler should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Snuggler,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Snuggler players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Snuggler must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-
set recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not
guarantee it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Snuggler, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Snuggler operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Snuggler, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Snuggler bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: use the bond as relationship evidence
only; never assign negative or positive alignment weight merely because players are bonded.

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Snuggler, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the
same wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An
opaque omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Snuggler only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when
called; observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw
Point once; Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception:
Before Hour 1, Snuggler chooses two distinct other players to become Snuggle-Bonded. The bonded
players learn each other’s identity as partners but not each other’s roles. Bond status is player-
bound. If one bonded player is caught by vote or a valid Hunt Mark, the other becomes Bond-Revealed
in the final resolution; Bond-Reveal exposes identity/current card for results but is not itself a
Paw Point capture.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Snuggler is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Snuggler's largest expert trap is a bond can connect Raider to a Business player, connect two
Business players who then overtrust each other, or become strategically irrelevant if neither bonded
player is pressured. The dossier intentionally keeps multiple plausible explanations alive long
enough for discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Snuggler should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Snuggler, specific balance anchor: creates a human trust channel that is mechanically real but
alignment-neutral, making social alliances less diagnostic and producing useful ambiguity without
adding another hostile faction. Specific failure risk: a bond can connect Raider to a Business
player, connect two Business players who then overtrust each other, or become strategically
irrelevant if neither bonded player is pressured. Playtests should record both, not just whether the
role's side won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Snuggler tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid
window, wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action
when legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and
unconverted branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Snuggler also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but
it must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Snuggler follows 1
d12. Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At each earlier wake the treats are
PRESENT; at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes see MISSING. The Snuggler player
records only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Snuggler player uses this role doctrine: Do not present the bond as a
clear. If revealing it, explain exactly what it proves: two people were linked before the night and
know each other as partners. It says nothing about Raider status or later conversion. Other players
provide competing claims. One player changes a wake story, another has a claim that conflicts with a
Business inspection, and Trouble reports a blind swap. The table distinguishes historical facts from
dawn state rather than concluding that every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Snuggler's
vote goal is: vote normally toward the historical Raider. Consider the secondary Bond-Reveal
consequence because catching one bonded player can expose the other, but do not treat exposure as
automatic primary victory unless another rule also catches the Raider.. All eligible tied leaders
are caught. Secondary bond/mark effects resolve, then the game checks whether the historical Raider
is in the final primary catch set. The replay can explain every transition without exposing facts
that were hidden during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Snuggler is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, Before Hour 1, Snuggler chooses two distinct other players to become Snuggle-Bonded.
The bonded players learn each other’s identity as partners but not each other’s roles. Bond status
is player-bound. If one bonded player is caught by vote or a valid Hunt Mark, the other becomes
Bond-Revealed in the final resolution; Bond-Reveal exposes identity/current card for results but is
not itself a Paw Point capture. Strategically, its strongest advantage is creates a human trust
channel that is mechanically real but alignment-neutral, making social alliances less diagnostic and
producing useful ambiguity without adding another hostile faction. Its balancing vulnerability is a
bond can connect Raider to a Business player, connect two Business players who then overtrust each
other, or become strategically irrelevant if neither bonded player is pressured.

From Cheese Thief, Snuggler inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Snuggler's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Snuggler, first list the confirmed
private facts available under the role contract: the selected bonded pair; own normal wake evidence;
no alignment information about the pair unless obtained elsewhere. Next list the strongest truthful
public claim, the strongest plausible hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question
another player can ask that may distinguish them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using
this role's objective: vote normally toward the historical Raider. Consider the secondary Bond-
Reveal consequence because catching one bonded player can expose the other, but do not treat
exposure as automatic primary victory unless another rule also catches the Raider.. The purpose of
this exercise is to train the distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive
narrative. A story can be legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every
known fact. The facilitator should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final
explanation.

Practice case 10: tie at Paw Point. two or more unprotected eligible players share the highest total
and are caught together, creating a controlled multi-suspect resolution. For Snuggler, first list

Analytical drill 1 for Snuggler. Build a deterministic night in which one confirmed fact supports
two different explanations. Use the role's core mechanic—Before Hour 1, Snuggler chooses two
distinct other players to become Snuggle-Bonded. The bonded players learn each other’s identity as
partners but not each other’s roles. Bond status is player-bound. If one bonded player is caught by
vote or a valid Hunt Mark, the other becomes Bond-Revealed in the final resolution; Bond-Reveal
exposes identity/current card for results but is not itself a Paw Point capture.—and combine it with
exactly one of these uncertainties: card movement, Hunter conversion, Trouble conversion, Guardian
protection, a Snuggle Bond, or a tied Paw Point. Ask players to write a timeline before naming a
suspect. Then ask them to mark every sentence as FACT, CLAIM, or INFERENCE. The drill passes only if
two legal narratives exist before discussion and at least one additional public statement can
distinguish them. This is the intended Ferret Frenzy texture: uncertainty constrained by rules
rather than randomness without structure.


ROLE DEEP DIVE: HUNTER
======================

Role charter. Hunter is one of exactly eight base roles and remains mechanically recognizable from
Revision 2.0. Team at setup: starts Business side and may convert to Bandit side. Wake structure: 2
distinct d12s. d6 use: on first overlap with any starting Bandit wake, roll once; 1–2 converts, 3–6
remains loyal. The solidification pass does not replace this role with an Among Us or Cheese Thief
role; it uses those games as design references for how evidence, deception, conversion, and voting
should feel. The authoritative mechanic is: Hunter and Guardian recognize each other before Hour 1.
Hunter wakes twice. On the first wake that overlaps any starting Bandit’s scheduled wake, Hunter
rolls d6 once; 1–2 creates a permanent player-bound Bandit-side conversion. After Hunter’s later
chronological wake, a still-loyal Hunter places one Hunt Mark. If loyal Hunter is caught in final
vote resolution, the mark catches its target only if that target is Bandit-aligned at resolution.
Converted Hunter loses Hunt Mark effect.


1. Immutable mechanical contract
--------------------------------

The first responsibility of the Hunter rules is to separate what is immutable from what can change
during the night. Starting Role is immutable and determines which scheduled role action the player
performs. Current Card is movable through Dooker or Trouble. Current Allegiance is a distinct state
that may depend on Current Card plus player-bound statuses. Historical Raider, Trouble accomplice
status, Hunter conversion, Snuggle Bond, Guardian protection, and Hunt Mark are player-bound or
event-bound facts and never jump merely because a card changes seats. For Hunter, the core action
contract is: two observations, one possible conversion check, and one conditional mark if still
loyal

This separation matters because Ferret Frenzy deliberately combines Cheese Thief's chronological
evidence with one-night card-state uncertainty. A player can accurately describe what happened at
Hour 4 while holding a different card at dawn. The game should reward players who can say 'this was
true then' instead of flattening the night into one final identity. For Hunter, confirmed private
facts include Guardian identity at setup; two wake windows and observations; own conversion result;
Hunt Mark target if loyal. Guardian never receives Hunter’s conversion result.. A claim about
anything beyond those facts is an inference or bluff, not a mechanically certified statement.

The role must never gain an undocumented secondary power through UI convenience. A button, bot
heuristic, quick-claim menu, or replay label cannot silently reveal information that the tabletop
rule would not provide. The conversion check occurs only once, at the first chronological overlap
with any starting Bandit wake. Later overlaps do not reroll. A moved Bandit Current Card does not
retroactively create an overlap; the trigger uses starting-Bandit schedules to preserve determinism.


2. Cheese Thief influence on the role
-------------------------------------

The heaviest Cheese Thief influence on Hunter is conditional allegiance change triggered by sharing
the relevant crime network resembles the design value of Followers: the night can change who
benefits from which public story. Ferret Frenzy expands the six-hour idea to twelve Burrow Hours
because several roles wake more than once, but it keeps the design purpose: a die result is not a
generic chance check; it locates a player in a chronology. That chronology should be discussable in
plain language after Hour 12.

Cheese Thief works because object state creates a shared temporal anchor. Ferret Frenzy's Minnow
Treats serve the same systemic function. PRESENT is not proof of innocence and MISSING is not proof
of guilt. Instead, each observation constrains when the raid could have happened. The strongest
deduction happens when multiple honest claims overlap: one player saw PRESENT, another saw MISSING,
and a third confirms who was awake between those moments. The role's special ability should add
texture to that network, not make it irrelevant.

For Hunter, a healthy Cheese-Thief-style round therefore asks: what did this role know because of
its own wake, what could it know only because it was alone or collided with somebody, and what
changed when the theft occurred? If a playtest shows that Hunter can ignore the Burrow Clock
entirely and still solve or dominate the round, the role is too detached from the core game.


3. Among Us influence on the role
---------------------------------

The strongest Among Us lesson for Hunter is the strongest influence is evidence invalidation. A fact
like “Guardian knows this was Hunter” remains true while the inference “therefore Hunter is still
safe” may become false.. In that design tradition, evidence is rarely useful in isolation. A
sighting becomes meaningful because it interacts with timing, route plausibility, role exceptions,
and what people later claim. Ferret Frenzy should create the same argumentative texture with hours
instead of rooms.

Morning Business is effectively the combat phase for Hunter. The night generates ammunition:
timestamps, co-wakers, Treat State, card movement, conversions, bonds, and limited role information.
The player then has to communicate those facts under uncertainty. A mechanically strong Hunter
should still be capable of poor play through bad disclosure, overconfidence, or a vote that ignores
contradictory evidence; a mechanically weak-looking Hunter should still be capable of excellent play
by organizing the table's information.

Special-role design must preserve interpretation. If an ability says only BANDIT-ALIGNED or NOT
BANDIT-ALIGNED, the game must not render that as RAIDER or SAFE. If recognition happened before a
possible conversion, the UI and rules must preserve the timestamp. If a card moved, the game must
distinguish the card's new seat from the person who acted earlier. These exceptions are not clutter;
they are the deduction space.


4. Evidence budget and information categories
---------------------------------------------

Hunter's evidence budget is intentionally bounded. Direct personal facts are: Guardian identity at
setup; two wake windows and observations; own conversion result; Hunt Mark target if loyal. Guardian
never receives Hunter’s conversion result.. Everything else belongs to one of four lower-confidence
categories: corroborated claims from another player, uncorroborated claims, deductions from timing,
or social reads. The dossier uses these categories because players and bots must not flatten them
into a single suspicion number.

A confirmed fact can still become stale. For example, an allegiance scan can be perfectly accurate
at Hour 5 and wrong as a description of dawn if a later card movement or conversion changes the
target. A player identity observed in a wake window remains historically accurate, but the inference
that the player was Raider may be wrong if several co-wakers were present. A starting-role
recognition remains accurate forever as history even if current allegiance later changes.

The practical rule for Hunter is to timestamp every fact mentally or in the private notebook. Say
'at my Hour 6 wake the treats were missing' rather than 'the theft happened before 6' unless the
second statement follows from all known evidence. This discipline makes Hunter stronger without
increasing its mechanical power.


5. Wake-window strategy
-----------------------

Wake count is one of Hunter's defining balance levers: 2 distinct d12s. A wake is not merely a turn
to press a power. It is a sample from the hidden night. The player should notice Treat State before
action, co-wakers, whether the theft changes state during the window, and whether the role's own
effect occurs before or after another effect in the same hour.

The twelve-hour clock should encourage comparative statements. Early PRESENT observations push the
theft later. Late MISSING observations pull it earlier. A co-waker creates mutual witness pressure
because each player may later claim the other was or was not present. Multiple wakes increase
coverage but also increase the number of claims an opponent can test for consistency.

For Hunter, the preferred discipline is to keep the raw schedule private until disclosure has
strategic value. Players are allowed to reveal it during Morning Business, but automatic public
publication would remove bluffing. A role with several wakes should not receive public credibility
simply because its story is complicated; it should earn credibility by matching independent
evidence.


6. Treat State and theft-window reasoning
-----------------------------------------

The Minnow Treat state is the common language shared by Hunter and every other role. At the
beginning of a wake window the player sees PRESENT or MISSING. If the historical Raider acts in that
window, authorized witnesses can also know that the state changed during that window. The rules
should never turn ordinary MISSING into an exact Raider reveal.

Public deduction should build an interval. Suppose one credible player woke at Hour 3 and saw
PRESENT, while another woke at Hour 7 and saw MISSING. The raid lies after the first observation and
no later than the second, subject to same-window order. Add a third credible claim at Hour 5 and the
interval can tighten. Add Trouble conversion or Hunter overlap and allegiance may change without
moving the historical crime.

Hunter's strategic contribution is strongest when the player states the observation before the
conclusion. This reduces false certainty and makes later contradictions legible. It also gives
Bandits something concrete they must imitate, which is healthier than a discussion based only on
personality reads.


7. Co-waker logic and witness topology
--------------------------------------

Co-wakers are not automatic allies or enemies. For Hunter, seeing another player awake proves that
person had a wake in the same window, unless a future expansion explicitly adds fake-wake mechanics.
It does not by itself prove role or allegiance. If the theft occurs in that window, the co-waker set
becomes much more important because the Raider is among the participants in the event.

Cheese Thief's strongest rounds are created by a web of who saw whom. Ferret Frenzy should preserve
that web even with special roles. A Business can confirm a claimed hour. Dooker can have three
separate witness sets. Hunter can overlap a starting Bandit and trigger conversion. Trouble can
become an accomplice by witnessing the theft. The topology of collisions is therefore a mechanical
resource.

For Hunter, players should avoid the common error 'I saw X awake, therefore X is guilty.' The useful
questions are narrower: did X's claimed role plausibly have a wake here, was the Treat State
compatible with X's story, did another witness confirm X, and does any known card movement explain
why X's current role claim differs from the wake behavior?


8. Starting Role, Current Card, and current allegiance
------------------------------------------------------

Hunter is played from Starting Role for scheduled actions. That rule prevents time paradoxes: if
Trouble moves a card after its owner already acted, the earlier action does not disappear, and if
Dooker places a new card onto someone before a later wake, the player still follows the starting-
role action schedule already locked at setup. Current Card matters to dawn state and allegiance
rules, but it does not rewrite history.

This structure borrows the useful uncertainty of one-night card movers while using an Among Us-like
evidence model: a role exception changes what a signal means. Someone may truthfully say 'I started
as Guardian' while holding Business at dawn. Someone may be historical Raider while no longer
holding Bandit. A converted Hunter may still be recognized by Guardian as the same player who
started Hunter.

Hunter's dossier logic therefore uses three separate questions: What action was this player entitled
to take? What card sits with this player now? What side does this player currently want to win?
Conflating those questions is one of the largest sources of rules bugs and bad bot behavior.


9. Morning Business opening doctrine
------------------------------------

Recommended opening for Hunter: Separate immutable truth from current conclusion: “Guardian and I
recognized each other before Hour 1” is a fact; “I am still loyal” is a claim only Hunter can
directly know. A loyal Hunter should use both wake windows to build an independent case rather than
demanding unconditional trust. The goal of the first statement is not to win the argument
immediately. It is to put falsifiable structure on the table. Among Us discussions are strongest
when players report observable sequence before jumping to a suspect; the same principle applies
here.

A useful opening follows the order HOUR -> TREAT STATE -> CO-WAKERS -> ROLE-SPECIFIC FACT ->
DEDUCTION. This order gives other players maximum opportunity to corroborate or contradict each
layer. Hostile players can use the same format, which is intentional: a game of deduction needs a
credible language for lying.

Hunter should also distinguish voluntary disclosure from forced disclosure. Direct questions can
pressure the player, but the app never auto-verifies a public role claim. The Quick Role Claim
system is a communication aid, not a lie detector.


10. Claim construction and bluff resistance
-------------------------------------------

A plausible public Hunter story can involve can truthfully claim Guardian recognition even after
conversion. This is deliberately dangerous because a once-valid trust relationship can become
stale.. The dossier does not prohibit lying; deception is part of the game. What it prohibits is the
software accidentally certifying a lie or exposing hidden information through button availability,
timing, bot phrasing, or reconnect behavior.

Truthful players should build claims from the smallest set of facts needed to advance deduction.
Revealing every private fact immediately can let Bandits tailor a perfect cover. Holding everything
until the final seconds can prevent the Business side from combining evidence. Good play is
therefore partly about disclosure timing.

Bluff resistance comes from cross-checks, not from making Hunter impossible to imitate. A hostile
player claiming Hunter should be forced to maintain a coherent schedule, Treat State, co-waker
story, and role-action story. The more independent witnesses exist, the more expensive an invented
narrative becomes.


11. Cross-examination questions
-------------------------------

When evaluating another player's story, Hunter should prefer precise questions: 'Which exact Burrow
Hour are you claiming?', 'Were the Minnow Treats present at the start of that wake?', 'Who else was
awake?', 'Did your role action happen before or after the theft in that hour?', and 'Are you
describing Starting Role or Current Card?' These questions expose mechanical contradictions without
requiring aggressive social pressure.

For a multi-wake claimant, ask whether they are disclosing all wakes or only one. For a player
affected by Trouble or Dooker, ask whether the claim refers to what they started as or what they
believe they hold now. For Hunter/Guardian, ask whether recognition is being offered as historical
fact or as current-allegiance proof.

Hunter should treat refusal to answer as social information only, not mechanical guilt. A player may
withhold because disclosure would help Bandits. The strongest contradiction is one that violates a
known wake, Treat State, role count, or card-movement rule.


12. Deception, omission, and partial truth
------------------------------------------

The key bluff lesson for Hunter is that partial truth is usually stronger than total fabrication.
two wake windows, a genuine trust anchor, and a conditional anti-Bandit retaliation tool when loyal.
A lie that preserves real wake timing is harder to disprove than a lie that requires multiple
players not to remember who shared an hour.

Among Us demonstrates that hostile narratives often succeed by changing interpretation rather than
inventing every observation. Ferret Frenzy should encourage the same. A Bandit may admit being awake
near the theft but argue that another co-waker acted. A converted Trouble may truthfully reveal the
card swap while omitting conversion. A converted Hunter may truthfully rely on Guardian's
recognition while lying about present loyalty.

For a Business-side Hunter, this lesson matters defensively: do not assume a truthful detail clears
the whole speaker. For hostile use of Hunter, do not assume one lie requires every other sentence to
be false.


13. Paw Point strategy
----------------------

Paw Point is one simultaneous locked vote per active player. There is no leader, mayor, sheriff, or
tie-break authority. Guardian protection is resolved before capture eligibility; all unprotected
eligible players tied for the highest remaining vote total are caught. For Hunter, the practical
objective is: loyal Hunter votes toward Raider and can strategically accept being caught if a high-
confidence Hunt Mark would add a Bandit-aligned target. Converted Hunter protects Raider and should
exploit Guardian’s stale trust without claiming information the role never received..

The simultaneous structure comes directly from the useful social-deduction principle that the vote
should reflect each player's final belief rather than a turn-order cascade. Players may discuss
intended votes, but the actual lock is private until resolution. Ties are meaningful because they
can catch multiple eligible suspects; this makes controlled split voting potentially useful to
Business and dangerous to Raider.

Hunter must also account for secondary effects. Snuggle Bond can add Bond-Reveal. Loyal Hunter can
add a conditional catch if Hunter itself was caught and the marked target is Bandit-aligned. These
effects should be forecast during discussion but never treated as if they changed who historically
stole the treats.


14. Guardian protection pressure
--------------------------------

Guardian's shield is a vote-resolution modifier, so every Hunter player should understand it even
when Guardian is not in the active deal. A protected target is removed from Paw Point capture
eligibility; the next-highest eligible vote total becomes decisive. This means protection can
redirect rather than merely cancel a capture.

Strategically, protection creates a second-order question: who benefits if the table's leading
suspect cannot be caught? If the leading suspect is Raider, a mistaken shield can hand the Bandit
side the round. If the leading suspect is a critical Business-side information role, a correct
shield can let the vote fall onto a better Raider candidate. The effect is strongest when the public
vote distribution is polarized.

For Hunter, do not confuse being protected with being cleared. Guardian may be wrong, deceived, or
acting on stale Hunter trust. The final dossier treats protection as procedural eligibility only,
never as evidence of alignment.


15. Dooker and Trouble card movement pressure
---------------------------------------------

Dooker and Trouble make card-state reasoning unavoidable. Dooker knows the Hammock card it may
insert but does not know the displaced player card. Trouble knows two positions swapped but neither
identity. When Hunter is one of those positions, the player's role action remains based on Starting
Role while Current Card may now imply a different dawn state.

This is where Ferret Frenzy most resembles modern evidence-contamination design: an observation can
be correct and still lead to the wrong current-state conclusion. The public discussion should
therefore carry timestamps. 'Dooker Glimpsed Alex Bandit-aligned at Hour 4' is materially different
from 'Alex is Bandit at dawn.' 'Trouble swapped Alex and Sam at Hour 7' gives a possible explanation
for later mismatches without telling anyone which card moved where.

For Hunter, the rule of thumb is never to erase earlier facts after movement. Add a new layer
instead. That makes the event log replayable and preserves the deduction chain.


16. Hunter conversion pressure
------------------------------

Hunter is a special source of stale trust. If Hunter shares the first applicable wake with a
starting Bandit, a private d6 of 1–2 converts Hunter. Guardian is intentionally not told. For
Hunter, this means a Guardian-Hunter mutual-recognition claim is strong evidence of starting
identities and weak evidence of current loyalty.

This design is heavily informed by both research sources: Cheese Thief demonstrates that Followers
can change alignment because of the night, while Among Us demonstrates that enabled roles can
invalidate what used to be a conclusive tell. The correct player skill is not memorizing a hidden
exception; it is asking whether the exception could have occurred in the claimed timeline.

Hunter should therefore ask whether Hunter's two wake windows could overlap a known or suspected
Bandit schedule. No one except Hunter receives the raw conversion result. Absence of proof is part
of the tension.


17. Snuggle Bond pressure
-------------------------

Snuggle Bond creates a real relationship with deliberately no alignment guarantee. For Hunter,
cooperation between bonded players is explainable without being exculpatory. The bond survives card
movement and conversion because it is attached to people, not cards.

The final resolution uses Bond-Reveal as a secondary exposure effect rather than a second vote. If
one partner is validly caught, the other can be exposed in results. The dossier keeps that effect
separate from primary Paw Point capture so that the bond creates information and consequence without
turning Snuggler into an accidental automatic Raider catcher.

Hunter players should use a bond claim to understand motive and communication, then return to
mechanical evidence. A bonded player may defend a partner out of relationship, shared information,
hostile coordination, or simple belief.


18. Small-lobby play
--------------------

In four- and five-player lobbies, every wake collision carries more weight because there are fewer
independent witnesses. Hunter must avoid overfitting one contradiction into certainty. The active-
set recipe still includes three Hammock cards, so the presence of a role in the pool does not
guarantee it is player-held.

Cheese Thief's player-count-specific structure is an important warning: scaling a social-deduction
game is not just removing seats. Ferret Frenzy keeps one core ruleset, but balance recipes should
control how many Bandit cards and high-information specials enter the Active Set. At low counts, a
single hard scan or convert can dominate if too many specials are guaranteed player-held.

For Hunter, the low-count strategy is to value each direct observation more while being explicit
about uncertainty. A clean two-person contradiction may be decisive, but card movement and
conversion still exist and must be checked before the group treats it as binary guilt.


19. Large-lobby play
--------------------

At eight to ten players, Hunter operates inside a denser claim network. More players mean more
potential corroboration but also more opportunities for Bandit-side coordination, Trouble/Hunter
conversion, bonds, and contradictory memories. The twelve-hour clock reduces constant collisions but
does not eliminate them.

Large lobbies should not solve themselves through role claims. Multiple players may plausibly claim
Business, some specials may sit in the Hammock, and Current Cards may have moved. The table should
organize by time first, then by role. A useful shared discussion order is Hours 1–4, 5–8, 9–12,
followed by card movement and special facts.

For Hunter, information discipline matters more than volume. The player who can summarize three
consistent claims is often more useful than the player who posts ten suspicions. Bot speech should
follow the same rule to avoid drowning humans in chatter.


20. Bot doctrine
----------------

A Hunter bot must play the same information game as a human. Its private state may contain
authoritative facts, but its chat layer can speak only from facts the role is entitled to know plus
deductions derived from public claims. Core bot objective: track conversion as a private player-
bound state; never leak it through role-action availability or chat phrasing. Converted bot stops
using loyal Hunt Mark logic..

The bot must preserve hidden-role privacy even when directly questioned. It may choose to volunteer
a role claim, but a user message cannot force the implementation to leak the bot's true hidden role.
Likewise, role-specific action availability cannot alter chat wording in a way that makes the role
obvious before the bot intentionally claims it.

For Hunter, post-game explainability is mandatory. A bot vote should be reconstructable from the
same wake, Treat State, claim contradiction, relationship, and role facts a human could cite. An
opaque omniscient suspicion score would undermine the social-deduction model.


21. New-player teaching order
-----------------------------

Teach Hunter only after teaching the common skeleton: roll private d12 wake hours; wake when called;
observe Treat State and co-wakers; Raider steals at its hour; discuss after Hour 12; Paw Point once;
Business wins by catching the historical Raider. Then add the role-specific exception: Hunter and
Guardian recognize each other before Hour 1. Hunter wakes twice. On the first wake that overlaps any
starting Bandit’s scheduled wake, Hunter rolls d6 once; 1–2 creates a permanent player-bound Bandit-
side conversion. After Hunter’s later chronological wake, a still-loyal Hunter places one Hunt Mark.
If loyal Hunter is caught in final vote resolution, the mark catches its target only if that target
is Bandit-aligned at resolution. Converted Hunter loses Hunt Mark effect.

This order follows both source games. Cheese Thief is teachable because the time system is
understandable before faction wrinkles. Among Us roles work because players first understand the
baseline behaviors that the role modifies. Ferret Frenzy should not explain Current Card,
conversion, bond, protection, and Glimpse all at once to a new player unless those effects are
actually in the Active Set.

The one-sentence teaching check for Hunter is: can the player explain what the role knows, when it
acts, and what it does not know? If not, reduce tutorial wording rather than adding more examples to
the live prompt.


22. Expert-play traps
---------------------

Experienced players are vulnerable to a different error: treating familiar patterns as proof.
Hunter's largest expert trap is Hunter can become the game’s most convincing compromised player
because Guardian can truthfully vouch for the starting relationship while lacking conversion
information. The dossier intentionally keeps multiple plausible explanations alive long enough for
discussion to matter.

Meta-gaming can also corrupt evidence. A player who says 'Alex always lies as Bandit' is using out-
of-round behavior rather than the current evidence graph. Social reads are allowed, but balance
testing should not assume a stable friend group. Bots and public lobbies need mechanics that work
when players have no history together.

Experts should also resist role-count certainty. The Active Set is player count plus three Hammock
cards drawn from the fixed pool recipe. A unique role existing in the recipe does not mean a human
necessarily holds it, and a public claim can be false even when no counterclaim appears.


23. Balance metrics
-------------------

Balance for Hunter should be measured by more than win rate. Track how often the role receives
actionable evidence, how often its power directly names or clears the Raider, how many distinct
public narratives remain plausible at the start of Morning Business, and whether the role causes
players to ignore the Treat State timeline.

A healthy round usually begins Morning Business with several plausible stories and ends with fewer
after discussion. If the role collapses the puzzle before discussion, reduce precision. If the role
adds only noise with no way to falsify stories, strengthen its connection to wake or Treat State. If
the role's optimal play is to remain silent every round, increase the value of selective disclosure
rather than simply buffing power.

For Hunter, specific balance anchor: two wake windows, a genuine trust anchor, and a conditional
anti-Bandit retaliation tool when loyal. Specific failure risk: Hunter can become the game’s most
convincing compromised player because Guardian can truthfully vouch for the starting relationship
while lacking conversion information. Playtests should record both, not just whether the role's side
won.


24. Deterministic QA cases
--------------------------

Every build should include seeded Hunter tests. Minimum cases: wake before raid, wake in raid
window, wake after raid, lone wake, crowded wake, Current Card moved before/after the role action
when legal, Guardian-protected vote target, Snuggle-Bond interaction, Hunter converted and
unconverted branches, and a tied Paw Point result.

For each case, assert five layers separately: authoritative hidden state; facts delivered to the
player; facts delivered to other clients; public claims entered by humans/bots; and final
resolution. A test fails if the correct final winner is reached through leaked information, because
privacy is part of gameplay correctness.

Hunter also needs a reconnect case. Reconnection may rebuild current authorized private facts, but
it must not replay another player's raw d6/d12, hidden prompt, or action animation. Historical event
logs may exist server-side without becoming player-visible.


25. Canonical example round
---------------------------

Example: build an eight-seat Active Set with two Bandit cards plus Business and specials, shuffle
one card to each player, and place three in the Hammock. The player who starts Hunter follows 2
distinct d12s. Assume the historical Raider is scheduled for Hour 6. At each earlier wake the treats
are PRESENT; at Hour 6 the Raider changes them to MISSING; later wakes see MISSING. The Hunter
player records only facts they are entitled to receive.

During Morning Business, the Hunter player uses this role doctrine: Separate immutable truth from
current conclusion: “Guardian and I recognized each other before Hour 1” is a fact; “I am still
loyal” is a claim only Hunter can directly know. A loyal Hunter should use both wake windows to
build an independent case rather than demanding unconditional trust. Other players provide competing
claims. One player changes a wake story, another has a claim that conflicts with a Business
inspection, and Trouble reports a blind swap. The table distinguishes historical facts from dawn
state rather than concluding that every mismatch is a lie.

Before Paw Point, Guardian may protect one other player. Votes then lock simultaneously. Hunter's
vote goal is: loyal Hunter votes toward Raider and can strategically accept being caught if a high-
confidence Hunt Mark would add a Bandit-aligned target. Converted Hunter protects Raider and should
exploit Guardian’s stale trust without claiming information the role never received.. All eligible
tied leaders are caught. Secondary bond/mark effects resolve, then the game checks whether the
historical Raider is in the final primary catch set. The replay can explain every transition without
exposing facts that were hidden during play.


26. Role doctrine summary
-------------------------

The final identity of Hunter is not its theme text; it is the pattern of decisions it creates.
Mechanically, Hunter and Guardian recognize each other before Hour 1. Hunter wakes twice. On the
first wake that overlaps any starting Bandit’s scheduled wake, Hunter rolls d6 once; 1–2 creates a
permanent player-bound Bandit-side conversion. After Hunter’s later chronological wake, a still-
loyal Hunter places one Hunt Mark. If loyal Hunter is caught in final vote resolution, the mark
catches its target only if that target is Bandit-aligned at resolution. Converted Hunter loses Hunt
Mark effect. Strategically, its strongest advantage is two wake windows, a genuine trust anchor, and
a conditional anti-Bandit retaliation tool when loyal. Its balancing vulnerability is Hunter can
become the game’s most convincing compromised player because Guardian can truthfully vouch for the
starting relationship while lacking conversion information.

From Cheese Thief, Hunter inherits respect for a random but reconstructable chronology. From Among
Us, it inherits respect for ambiguous evidence and discussion as the decisive contest. The role is
successful when players can argue about what its evidence means without arguing about what the rules
literally did.

Implementation shorthand: preserve Starting Role; timestamp private facts; keep Current Card and
allegiance separate; never auto-verify public claims; use d12 only for wake hours and d6 only for
defined chance checks; preserve the historical Raider; and make Hunter's public power come from
reasoning, not from hidden UI leaks.


27. Practice-case library
-------------------------

Practice case 1: early raid. Raider acts in Hours 1–3, so most later players see MISSING and the
burden is finding the last credible PRESENT observation. For Hunter, first list the confirmed
private facts available under the role contract: Guardian identity at setup; two wake windows and
observations; own conversion result; Hunt Mark target if loyal. Guardian never receives Hunter’s
conversion result.. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest plausible hostile
reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may distinguish them.
Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: loyal Hunter votes toward
Raider and can strategically accept being caught if a high-confidence Hunt Mark would add a Bandit-
aligned target. Converted Hunter protects Raider and should exploit Guardian’s stale trust without
claiming information the role never received.. The purpose of this exercise is to train the
distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be
legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator
should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 2: late raid. Raider acts in Hours 10–12, so many players see PRESENT and late
witnesses become disproportionately important. For Hunter, first list the confirmed private facts
available under the role contract: Guardian identity at setup; two wake windows and observations;
own conversion result; Hunt Mark target if loyal. Guardian never receives Hunter’s conversion
result.. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest plausible hostile
reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may distinguish them.
Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: loyal Hunter votes toward
Raider and can strategically accept being caught if a high-confidence Hunt Mark would add a Bandit-
aligned target. Converted Hunter protects Raider and should exploit Guardian’s stale trust without
claiming information the role never received.. The purpose of this exercise is to train the
distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be
legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator
should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 3: crowded theft window. three or more players share the theft hour, creating direct
witness pressure but also multiple plausible speakers who can redirect blame. For Hunter, first list
the confirmed private facts available under the role contract: Guardian identity at setup; two wake
windows and observations; own conversion result; Hunt Mark target if loyal. Guardian never receives
Hunter’s conversion result.. Next list the strongest truthful public claim, the strongest plausible
hostile reinterpretation of that claim, and one question another player can ask that may distinguish
them. Finally resolve the Paw Point consequences using this role's objective: loyal Hunter votes
toward Raider and can strategically accept being caught if a high-confidence Hunt Mark would add a
Bandit-aligned target. Converted Hunter protects Raider and should exploit Guardian’s stale trust
without claiming information the role never received.. The purpose of this exercise is to train the
distinction between a mechanically possible narrative and a persuasive narrative. A story can be
legal yet badly supported; another can sound suspicious yet fit every known fact. The facilitator
should not reveal hidden state until everyone has committed to a final explanation.

Practice case 4: solo information window. the role wakes without co-wakers and must decide how much
